# Changelog

All notable changes to the Orleans.Lattice package family are documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/), and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

This changelog covers the whole **package family** - every published `Orleans.Lattice` and `Orleans.Lattice.*` package, spanning the core library and its replication, storage, membership, auth, data, backup, caching, schema, scaling, dashboards, MCP, API/gRPC binding, and Explorer companions. Packages ship in lockstep on the major and minor digits; patch digits may advance per-package.

This is the **v9.x** changelog. Earlier release lines are archived: v8.x in [`CHANGELOG.old.v8.md`](CHANGELOG.old.v8.md), v7.x in [`CHANGELOG.old.v7.md`](CHANGELOG.old.v7.md), and v6.x and earlier in [`CHANGELOG.old.v6.md`](CHANGELOG.old.v6.md).

## Unreleased

Outstanding work is tracked on [GitHub Issues](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues), labelled per project. See [`docs/RELEASING.md`](docs/RELEASING.md) for the per-package tag-and-publish protocol.

### Added

- **Repository-context memory records can now be claimed under a fenced, expiring lease, which makes an agent-operated backlog safe for several agents to drain at once.** The backlog itself is a set of memory entries under a `backlog` topic, mirrored to GitHub issues so nothing can be enqueued invisibly, with dependencies, code anchors and groupings carried as typed links rather than as a schema the store cannot enforce. The hazard that mechanism had to solve is specific: memory records merge without coordination, so an edge asserting "this run owns this item" is an audit record of who tried rather than a lock, and the obvious fix - a boolean `claimed` flag - fails in exactly the case that matters, because a scheduled agent session can be stopped at any instant and a flag set by a killed session strands its item forever with no way to tell a live owner from a dead one. Four new tools address both by wrapping the cluster's existing distributed lock instead of reimplementing mutual exclusion: `repocontext_claim` takes a record for exclusive authorship and returns the lock's strictly increasing fencing token under a bounded lease, `repocontext_renew_claim` extends that lease without changing the token, `repocontext_release_claim` hands the record to the next waiter, and the read-only `repocontext_claim_status` reports who holds an item and how deep the queue is. Exclusion, first-in-first-out fairness, expiry reclaim and token monotonicity are all the lock's, so `Claimed -> Ready` on lease expiry is the normal path rather than an exception. Crucially the token is enforced rather than advertised: `repocontext_remember`, `repocontext_update` and `repocontext_forget` each evaluate the fence on the write path itself and refuse a write that does not admit, covering the record's `body` and not merely its edges, so a superseded holder that still believes it owns an item is refused instead of trusted. The fencing high-water mark is stored as a bounded register keyed by the token itself, making it a join-semilattice maximum that no lower token can displace by a direct write or by a concurrent replica merge - a trustworthy high-water mark without a compare-and-swap the store does not have - and claims are region-scoped and supported on memory records only, both fail-closed. An unclaimed record admits any write, so every pre-existing caller is unchanged; contention is reported (`granted: false` with a reason) rather than thrown, so a work-stealing agent branches on the result; and `repocontext_claim_status` reports `authoritative: false` by construction so no call site can gate a decision on a snapshot that may already be stale. Shipping alongside are the worker and project-manager agent protocols, the backlog data model, and an epic-branch convention under which a grouping's items share one `<type>/epic/<slug>` branch and reach `main` as a single gated pull request, so an epic does not pay quadratic CI serialising N pull requests through a strict required check. The protocol and both agent definitions ship as a repository-neutral template under `samples/AgentBacklog/template/`, and this repository consumes that template unmodified through two binding overrides of under seventy lines each, so an adopter gets a working backlog by copying one directory and supplying its own repository, account and region rather than by transcribing prose - and the template cannot rot into a stale copy, because if it is wrong this repository's own agents are wrong with it. Keeping the protocol there rather than in the always-applied instructions file also removes about four hundred lines from the context every session pays for, retaining only the two rules that bind every agent touching memory: that the backlog relations must never be pruned as unknown, and that a backlog item never carries a TTL. See [The agent-operated backlog](docs/lattice.api.mcp.repocontext/backlog.md). ([#2055](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2055)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **The Explorer console is now coherent, addressable and accessible.** Primary navigation has moved from four near-identical horizontal tab strips to a stable vertical left rail with four visually distinct tiers, so hierarchy is legible at a glance and no two adjacent tiers can share a label. Every view now has a lower-case URL, so deep links, bookmarks, sharing and browser back and forward all work, and what the console remembers is one documented, enumerated contract scoped per user and per cluster with a reset escape - the active area is restored on reload rather than silently forgotten. An area you cannot open stays visible but demoted and states the permission it needs and who to ask, instead of disappearing, greying out silently, or telling a signed-out visitor that a surface "is not available for your account"; the gating is advisory, the server remains the sole enforcement point, and every plugin gate now resolves one shared four-state contract that a reflection-based conformance test holds it to. Tenant scope is a first-class lens: switching tenant works (it was previously reverted within the same call by the identity resolver), the free-text tenant box is replaced by a picker that adapts to how many tenants you can actually reach, and a remembered tenant is re-validated fail-closed on restore rather than re-applied on the strength of having once been allowed. Theme, contrast and density are now yours to choose and are applied at first paint with no flash of the wrong palette, with light promoted to a genuinely first-class palette. The console targets WCAG 2.1 and 2.2 AA: non-text contrast now meets its floor in both palettes (borders were 1.21:1), state no longer rests on hue alone, the compact-width overflow menu is no longer clipped off the left edge of the viewport, and conformance is guarded by a browserless contrast check in the required build plus a widened browser sweep and journey suite. ([#1845](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/1845)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Explorer`)

- **The Explorer is now composed of plugins, and adapts from phone to desktop.** The console has been rewritten around a plugin contract: each area ships as its own package carrying its own view, the single domain contract that is the whole of its reach, and its own access gate, so a head surfaces an area by registering it and withholds it by not registering it - adding a tab needs no change to the shell, and there are no per-area option flags. The UI is fluent, reflowing between compact, medium and expanded from one named breakpoint set rather than a fixed desktop layout. Two tenancy surfaces arrive with it, split by privilege: platform operators manage tenant lifecycle, quota, region authorization and the initial admin grant, while tenant administrators manage their own membership, cross-tenant grants and region residency and see usage against a quota they cannot change. Tenant metrics are served by a new backend-neutral telemetry facade that derives every answer's tenant scope on the server and reports a narrowed request as narrowed. ([#1716](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/1716))

- **The repository-context index can now be sourced directly from a git ref, so a hub is anchored to a commit instead of to whatever someone mounted.** Until now the indexer's only source was a read-only bind mount: the truth about "what is indexed" lived outside the system, two hosts could mount divergent content, nothing recorded which revision was being served, and a delete was inferred from a file's absence on disk - so an unmounted or half-synced volume read as a mass deletion. Listing a repository in `LATTICE_REPOCONTEXT_GIT_REPOS` and giving it a `_URL` and a `_REF` switches it to a new opt-in, hub-only source strategy: the host fetches the ref into a staging work tree with LibGit2Sharp (managed, so no `git` binary is required), resolves it to a commit, and indexes that commit. If the resolved commit matches the one the last completed generation was stamped with, the pass is a no-op. Otherwise the exact add / modify / delete set is computed by diffing the commit's tree against the stored per-file digests and only that delta is applied, so deletes between commits are read from git rather than guessed from the filesystem, and no directory is walked at all. Every generation is stamped with its resolved commit, which `repocontext_list_repos` now reports as `indexedCommit` and which replicates to spokes with the rest of the index, so any replica can state the revision it is serving. The mounted workspace remains the default and is untouched; the two are mutually exclusive per repository, and a git-sourced repository is refused by `repocontext_add_repo` so a mount can never shadow the configured remote. The security posture is fail-closed throughout: credentials resolve per repository through a pluggable provider seam (the shipped one reads a per-repository `_TOKEN`, and there is deliberately no ambient shared token variable that one repository could use to fetch another's remote), a repository configured for token auth with no token does not index rather than falling back to an anonymous fetch or to a mount, anonymous access has to be asked for by name, and tokens are redacted from every log line and error message including a url's userinfo component. A fetch that fails, times out, or authenticates badly leaves the last-good index serving and prunes nothing, and a late or duplicated refresh costs at most one no-op fetch. Configuration covers the ref, shallow depth, refresh interval, fetch timeout, include and exclude globs, and binary exclusion; see [Index source strategies](docs/lattice.api.mcp.repocontext/container.md#index-source-strategies). ([#1550](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/1550)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

### Changed

- **Pre-ship CRDT delta coalescing allocates up to 80% less, by deduplicating on the element bytes themselves instead of on a throwaway base64 string.** The replication shipper coalesces its drain buffer before every ship, folding each key's run of pending deltas into a single wire delta through `CrdtShape.CombineDeltas`. That combine is a **pairwise running left fold**, so union number k re-walks everything unions 1 to k-1 accumulated: a per-element cost inside the union is quadratic in the run length rather than linear, and a run of a few dozen deltas is ordinary on a busy tree. Three output-identical reductions, all on `private static` helpers, touching no public API and no wire format. (1) The grow-only element union and the OR-Set and RW-Set delta-dot unions all deduplicated on a `Convert.ToBase64String(element)` surrogate. Base64 is injective over byte sequences, so sequence equality over the raw bytes states exactly the same equivalence - the surrogate bought nothing but a fresh string per element, per union, per fold step, re-encoding every already-accumulated element each time the fold advanced. Both now deduplicate on the element array itself through a structural comparer that hashes with `HashCode.AddBytes` and short-circuits on reference equality, which the fold hits constantly because the accumulator holds the very same array instances the next union is handed. The distinction between a null element and a legitimately empty one is preserved, and preserved more directly than before: a null element is skipped before it can reach the set, where the base64 form had to reason about `Convert.ToBase64String([])` being indistinguishable from `string.Empty`. This deliberately does **not** touch the base64 keys in `OrSet` and `RwSet` *state*, where the string genuinely is the stored key and is part of the persisted shape. (2) Every dedup accumulator in those helpers - the element set, the delta-dot set, and the RGA-insert and OR-Set-dot sets - grew from empty even though a union can never exceed its two sources' combined width, which is known before the first insert. All of them now presize from that bound, so a fold no longer walks the prime doubling chain rehashing what it already holds; presizing to zero costs nothing, because an empty `HashSet` and `List` allocate no backing storage. (3) The OR-Map add combine kept a `Dictionary<dot, entry>` alongside a `List<dot>` of wide dot tuples recording first-observation order, then ran a third pass that re-hashed every dot once more to materialise the ordered array. It now maps each dot to its **slot** in the result list, so order is carried by the list itself, the re-probe pass and the tuple order list are both gone, and the remaining probe is folded to one with `CollectionsMarshal.GetValueRefOrAddDefault`; the merge target is reached through `CollectionsMarshal.AsSpan` so the wide entry struct is not copied out to get at it. Clone-on-first-observation is unchanged, so a combine still never mutates a source delta's snapshot. Measured on a new `crdtcoalescetrims` BenchmarkDotNet suite whose baseline arms reproduce each optimised arm's surrounding fold shell exactly, so that only the body under test differs, with a third lane per CRDT folding the same estate through the real shipped `CrdtShape.ForXxx().CombineDeltas` to pin the copied shells to production, and a global setup that fails the run outright if any two arms of a lane disagree on the folded result. Allocation is deterministic and reproduced bit-identically in every round, so it is the figure led on. Folding a 24-delta run carrying 16 elements each: the grow-only element union allocates **80% less** (659.3 KB to 132.2 KB), with gen-0 collections down from 80.6 to 16.1 per thousand operations and gen-1 from 7.6 to 0.4; the OR-Set union **68% less** (1218.7 KB to 384.2 KB), gen-0 148.9 to 46.9 and gen-1 18.6 to 2.9; and the OR-Map combine **33% less** (2981.3 KB to 1995.7 KB), gen-0 363.3 to 244.1 and gen-1 117.2 to 72.8. Every production lane landed within 1.5 KB of its optimised arm, confirming the copied shells are faithful. A contrast arm measures the cheap half of the first two changes on its own - presizing the dedup set while keeping the base64 surrogate - and is why the structural comparer is there: 504.8 KB, a 23% saving against the shipped arm's 80%, so more than two thirds of the win is the surrogate elision and not the presize. Timings on this shared developer host carry standard deviations wide enough that they are reported as a direction tally rather than a headline: the optimised arm was faster in all three rounds in all three lanes, by 41-71% on the element union, 25-50% on the OR-Set union and 20-40% on the OR-Map combine, but a baseline mean moved by as much as 74% between rounds, so the magnitudes are indicative and the allocation figures are the measurement. (`Orleans.Lattice`)

- **The repository-context reconcile walk and the default replication apply path each shed a steady-state cost that was paid on every pass.** Three output-identical reductions, all inside `internal` types, touching no public API. (1) `ReplicationApplier.ApplyRunsSequentiallyAsync` is the apply path every received batch takes unless the parallel planner opts it out, so it is the one that actually runs by default. It segmented a batch into same-origin runs by reading the run head three times and each candidate three times through an `IReadOnlyList<WalRecord>` indexer; `WalRecord` is a wide `readonly record struct` carrying 26 serialized members, so every one of those reads copied the whole struct, and the JIT cannot elide the repeats across the interface call. A previous change fixed exactly this shape in the *parallel* planner and left the sequential path untouched. The scan is now a `private static` `FindRunEndExclusive` helper that binds the head and each candidate once and projects the run key off that single copy, so one struct copy per entry replaces three; lifting it out of the async method also keeps the run-key locals out of the generated state machine. Segmentation is unchanged, including the security-relevant property that `Mode` is part of the run key, so a prepared entry can never be collapsed into an unprepared run. (2) `RepoTreeWalker.GroupKnownByDirectory` and `GroupDirectoriesByParent` build the two per-directory indexes the pruned walk needs, so their cost is paid over the whole known corpus on every reconcile pass for the lifetime of the container. Both had the same two defects. They bucketed into a `Dictionary<string, List<T>>`, which for the overwhelmingly common directory costs three allocations - the list object, the four-slot array its first `Add` grows into from empty, and the doubling chain beyond that. And, more expensively, `GroupKnownByDirectory` cut a fresh parent-directory `string` out of every known file path with `Substring` purely to use it as a lookup key, so a corpus of N files across D directories allocated N throwaway strings to address D buckets. Both now run as two passes over a single dictionary whose value carries the counted width and the bucket together: the first pass counts, keyed by a `ReadOnlySpan<char>` slice of the path through the dictionary's `GetAlternateLookup<ReadOnlySpan<char>>()` so the parent substring is materialised once per *directory* rather than once per *file*, and the second allocates each bucket on its first touch at exactly the counted width and fills it forward, reusing the counted cell as the fill cursor so source order is preserved without a second cursor map. Holding the count and the bucket in one dictionary value is load-bearing rather than tidy: a first draft that kept a separate count map and a separate bucket map hashed each directory three times per file and measured consistently slower than the list baseline it replaced, despite allocating less, and folding them to two probes is what recovered the time. `GroupDirectoriesByParent` also took an `IEnumerable<string>` that a two-pass shape would enumerate twice; it now takes the `IReadOnlyDictionary<string, long>` its single call site always had. (3) `RepoContextBootstrapPlan.Compute`, the pure digest diff that partitions a scan into added, updated, unchanged and metadata-changed files, grew all four partitions from empty `List<RepoFileEntry>`s through their full doubling chains on every pass, even in the converged steady state where one partition holds the entire corpus and the other three are allocated and stay empty. It now classifies into a `byte[]` side buffer while counting the four widths in a `stackalloc`ed span, then allocates each partition once at its exact width, so an empty class allocates nothing at all; the prune list is likewise created lazily, so a converged tree allocates nothing for it either. Measured on a new `ingestapplytrims` BenchmarkDotNet suite whose baseline arms reproduce each optimised arm's surrounding shell exactly, so that only the body under test differs, and whose run-scan lane indexes through the same `IReadOnlyList<WalRecord>` interface the production path does, over three full-fidelity rounds on a shared developer host plus two further rounds for the contrast arm. Allocation is deterministic and reproduced bit-identically in every round, so it is the figure led on: grouping 4,096 files across 512 directories allocates **46% less** (972,576 B to 525,792 B), with gen-0 collections down from 115.7 to 62.0 per thousand operations and gen-1 from 44.9 to 24.9, and computing the plan for that same corpus in its all-unchanged steady state allocates **38% less** (425,233 B to 265,337 B). Neither of those two lanes has a trustworthy timing verdict on this host - the grouping baseline's own mean moved by 23% between rounds, wider than the difference being measured, and the optimised arm was slower in three rounds and faster in two - so both are reported as within noise and the case for them rests on allocation and on the halved collection counts. A contrast arm measures the cheaper half of the grouping change on its own, the span-keyed substring elision keeping the list buckets, and is why the second pass is there: one probe per item instead of two, but only **21% less** allocation (767,936 B) against the shipped arm's 46%, and no timing advantage that survives the noise. The run scan is allocation-free on both arms by construction and so is the one timing-only lane: it was faster in all three rounds, by 17.6%, 17.7% and 27.8%, with the optimised arm's standard deviation a fraction of the baseline's each time. That lane is also a warning worth recording - an earlier round on a busier host measured it 36% *slower*, so a sub-microsecond lane on shared hardware needs several rounds and a direction tally rather than a single run. (`Orleans.Lattice.Replication`, `Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **The tenancy snapshot rebuild allocates a quarter less, by sizing every index bucket exactly once instead of growing a list per entry.** The three tenancy snapshot maintainers rebuild their entire snapshot from the whole registry every time the tenant tree changes and again on their periodic refresh cadence, so this cost is paid over every tenant record in the store, repeatedly, for the lifetime of the silo. Three output-identical reductions, all inside `internal` types, touching no public API. (1) `CompiledTenantPolicy.Compile` inverted admin subjects into a `Dictionary<string, List<TenantId>>` accumulator and froze it through the **selector** `ToFrozenDictionary` overload. Because the overwhelmingly common subject administers exactly one tenant, that cost three allocations for every distinct subject - the list object, the four-slot backing array its first `Add` grows into from empty, and the exact-sized copy `ToArray` takes at freeze time - plus an intermediate the selector overload builds because it is not the `Dictionary`-source fast path. The inversion now runs as two passes over the already-compiled tenants: the first counts how many tenants each subject administers, the second allocates every bucket once at that exact width and fills it from the back, so the counted width doubles as the fill cursor and no second cursor map is needed. It deliberately does **not** take the grow-by-one `Array.Resize` append the authorization compile uses, because that append is only sound when bucket cardinality is bounded and small, and neither tenancy index has that bound - a shared platform-operator subject can administer every tenant in the estate. The tenant map is also presized from the source count when the source can hand it over without enumerating, which the maintainer's materialised list always can. (2) `CompileTenantGrants` had the identical list-bucket and selector-freeze shape per record and is replaced the same way, walking the grants in reverse so each grant still lands at its encounter position and the grant-id ordering `TenantRecord.Grants` hands over is preserved; a record with no tenant-grantee grant still allocates nothing at all. (3) `TenantPlacementSnapshot.Build` and `TenantResidencySnapshot.Build` each copied their source into a defensive dedup `Dictionary` before freezing, even though their only production caller has just scanned the registry into a dictionary and so already guarantees unique keys; a dictionary source now freezes directly, and any other source is deduplicated as before into a map presized from the source's own count where that is available for free. Measured on a new `tenancycompiletrims` BenchmarkDotNet suite whose baseline arms reproduce each optimised arm's surrounding shell exactly, so that only the body under test differs, with a third lane running the real shipped `CompiledTenantPolicy.Compile` to pin the copied shell to production - it allocated bit-identically to the optimised lane, confirming the copy is faithful. Allocation is deterministic and is the figure led on: a full compile of 256 tenants across a 320-subject pool allocates **26% less** (1437 KB to 1070 KB), the subject inversion 18% less, the grantee index 44% less at a narrow fan-in and 58% less at a wide one, the placement snapshot at least 44% less, and the residency snapshot at least 34% less, with gen-0 and gen-1 collections on the compile lane both down by roughly a quarter; every one of those figures reproduced bit-identically in both rounds except the two snapshot baselines, whose unsized growth chain lands slightly differently per run. A contrast arm measures the rejected grow-by-one append at the wide fan-in and confirms why it was rejected: 1034 KB against the count-then-fill arm's 10.5 KB, and 41 times the *baseline* it was meant to improve on. Timings on the shared host were too noisy to lead on - the compile lane's own baseline moved by less than 2% between rounds while its optimised arm moved by 63%, so that lane is reported as within noise rather than quantified. Two lanes were nonetheless faster in both rounds by a wide margin (the narrow grantee index, and both snapshot builds at roughly half the time), and one is honestly slower: at the pathological wide fan-in the count-then-fill grantee arm costs about 25-110% more time for its 58% allocation saving, because it hashes each grantee twice. That trade is deliberate - it is the arm that bounds the tail, where the alternative append is 10 times slower again and allocates 98 times more. (`Orleans.Lattice.Tenancy`)

- **The authorization policy compile now allocates roughly half of what it did, by holding rule buckets as arrays instead of lists.** `CompiledPolicySnapshotMaintainer` rebuilds the entire compiled snapshot from the whole ruleset every time the policy tree changes and again on its periodic refresh cadence, so the compile path's cost is paid over every rule in the store, repeatedly, for the lifetime of the silo. Three output-identical reductions, all inside the `internal` `CompiledTree`, touching no public API. (1) `Append` bucketed each scope's rules into a `List<CompiledRule>` and probed with `TryGetValue` before storing through the indexer on a miss. Because the overwhelmingly common scope carries exactly one rule, that cost three allocations for every distinct exact key and prefix - the list object, the four-slot backing array its first `Add` grows into from empty, and the exact-sized copy `ToArray` takes at freeze time - where one array would do. Buckets are now arrays, appended through a single `CollectionsMarshal.GetValueRefOrAddDefault` whose `existed` flag distinguishes creating a bucket from extending one; growth is O(k) per rule, but k is the number of rules sharing one exact key or prefix, which is one or two in any real policy. Declaration order, which decides ties in `TryBestInBucket`, is preserved. (2) The prefix index materialisation in `Build` sorted the key array on its own and then hashed every one of those keys back through the builder to fetch its bucket - a full dictionary lookup per prefix that the enumeration it had just finished already had in hand, plus a per-bucket `ToArray`. It now fills the key and rule arrays in a single pass and sorts them together as parallel arrays; the keys are unique, so the ordinal ordering that the longest-prefix binary search in `ResolvePoint` depends on is total and the paired sort is deterministic. (3) `Freeze` filled an intermediate `Dictionary` purely to change the bucket type from list to array, then discarded it the instant `ToFrozenDictionary` had copied the entries back out. With buckets already in their final array form it freezes the builder directly, which also keeps the `Dictionary`-source `ToFrozenDictionary` overload rather than the selector overload that builds an intermediate of its own. Measured on a new `authcompiletrims` BenchmarkDotNet suite whose baseline arms reproduce each optimised arm's surrounding shell exactly, so that only the body under test differs, with a third lane running the real shipped `CompiledPolicy.Compile` to pin the copied shell to production. Run at full fidelity over two rounds on a shared developer host. Allocation is deterministic and was reproduced bit-identically in both rounds, so it is the figure led on: a full compile of roughly 2,300 rules across 24 trees allocates **47% less** (983 KB to 517 KB, and the production lane 983 KB to 520 KB), the bucketing 36% less, the prefix index 78% less, and the freeze 47% less, with gen-0 and gen-1 collections on the compile lane both roughly halved. Every lane was also faster on the median in both rounds - the full compile by 33% and 32%, the prefix index by 45% and 29%, the freeze by 5% and 24%, and the bucketing by 51% in the quieter round - but the baseline means of the bucketing and freeze lanes varied by more than a factor of two between rounds, so those two timings are reported as directionally confirmed rather than precisely quantified, and the case for them rests on their allocation. (`Orleans.Lattice.Auth`)

- **Three more steady-state allocations and two redundant hash probes are trimmed from the replication apply leg.** All three sites are output-identical, internal, and touch no public API. (1) `ReplicationApplier.BuildParallelApplyPlanOrNull`, which scans a received batch for same-origin runs before deciding whether the trees it touches can be applied in parallel, read `entries[j]` three times per inner-loop iteration and `entries[i]` three times per run start through an `IReadOnlyList<WalRecord>` indexer. `WalRecord` is a wide `readonly record struct` carrying 26 serialized members, so every one of those reads copied the whole struct; the scan now binds one local per candidate and reads that, which is where most of the win is. Its per-tree group lookup also folds the previous `TryGetValue`-then-indexer-store pair onto a single `CollectionsMarshal.GetValueRefOrAddDefault` probe, hashing each tree id once instead of twice on a miss. (2) `ReplicationApplier.ApplyOriginRunAsync` grew its four deferred-apply accumulators - the CRDT delta bucket, the LWW merge bucket, and the two parallel index-and-sequence lists that pair with them - from empty, one element at a time, through the full doubling chain on every run that defers work. Each is now presized from the run's own length, which is an exact upper bound on how many entries that run can possibly defer, clamped at 256 so a long mostly-deduped or mostly-parked run cannot over-allocate on a bound it will never approach. The buckets stay lazily constructed, so a run that defers nothing still allocates nothing. (3) `LeafReReplayer.ApplyCaps`, which regroups a capped re-replay window back into whole atomic transactions so a transaction is never emitted torn, folds the same double hash probe onto one `GetValueRefOrAddDefault`, presizes each per-transaction member list from the batch's own declared `AtomicBatchSize` - its exact final width, not a hint - and presizes the emitted-transaction set from the group count. Measured on a new `replicationapplytrims` BenchmarkDotNet suite whose baseline arms reproduce each optimised arm's surrounding shell exactly, so that only the body under test differs, over three full-fidelity rounds on a shared developer host. Allocation is deterministic and reproduced bit-identically in every round, so it is the figure led on here: the run scan allocates 4% less, the deferred accumulators 25% less with a quarter fewer gen-0 and gen-1 collections, and the cap regrouping 15% less with gen-1 promotion eliminated outright. On the median of the three rounds the run scan is 15% faster and the cap regrouping 28% faster, both faster in all three rounds; the deferred accumulators are 10% faster on that median but faster in only two rounds of three, so that one lane's timing is reported as within noise and the case for it rests on its allocation. (`Orleans.Lattice.Replication`)

- **A converged repository-context repository no longer re-probes its whole corpus for embedding gaps on every reconcile.** Once a repository is structurally converged, a background pass has essentially no structural work left, yet it still offered every content-unchanged file to the vector ingestor, which probed two membership reads per indexed source to ask whether a vector had landed. On a 7892-file repository that is roughly 62 batched round trips into the membership tree per pass, and it was about 97% of a 400-second pass that found nothing, every pass, forever - load that also crowds out the very embedding work it is looking for. The whole-corpus probe is now on its own cadence, `LATTICE_EMBEDDING_GAP_SCAN_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (default four hours), counted in reconcile passes like the full walk. Nothing waits four hours to be healed: a repository that has never yet been observed gap-free is probed on every pass until it is, and the self-index grain's continuous out-of-band paged gap sweep - which is already the incremental, bounded probe this cost was paying for - now forces an immediate in-pass scan on the next reconcile the moment it finds a gap, through a new `ForceEmbeddingGapScan` flag threaded from the grain through the job grain and runner to the pass coordinator. That flag is load-bearing rather than cosmetic: both the periodic reconcile and the gap-triggered re-drive request pruning, so pruning alone cannot distinguish them, and without it a gap found while the in-pass scan was backed off would re-drive every few minutes and heal nothing until the cadence came round. The coordinator also takes a convergence verdict only from a pass that actually scanned, so a pass that was offered nothing cannot overwrite what the last real scan learned. Separately, the embedding batch loop now backs off after three consecutive batch failures instead of driving every remaining batch into a vector plane that has already proven saturated; a pass that embedded nothing and saw a failure still surfaces the fault rather than reporting a quiet success. `IRepoContextVectorIngestor.IngestAsync` now returns a `RepoFileVectorIngestOutcome` (embedded count, gaps selected, and whether coverage was actually established) instead of a bare count, so the coordinator can tell "scanned and found nothing" from "did not scan"; the seam is `internal`, so no public surface changes. ([#2049](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2049)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **Four steady-state allocations and one redundant hash probe are trimmed from the replication ship and apply legs.** All four sites are output-identical, internal, and touch no public API. (1) `VectorClockCodec.EncodeAbsolute`, which snapshots the causal frontier onto every shipped batch, grew its replacement map from empty one origin at a time; it now delegates to `VersionVector.Clone`, whose dictionary copy constructor presizes exactly and bulk-copies, so the grow-and-rehash chain is gone. (2) `VectorClockCodec.EncodeDelta` presizes its filtered result to the source entry count, a sound upper bound on a filter. (3) `VectorClockCodec.DecodeDelta`, which runs per applied batch on the receiver, reserves merge headroom with `EnsureCapacity` and folds the pointwise max through a single `CollectionsMarshal.GetValueRefOrAddDefault` probe instead of hashing each origin twice (`TryGetValue` then an indexer store). (4) `ContentManifestPlanner.BuildManifest` now counts eligible entries in a first pass - four field compares, no allocation - and allocates the manifest list at exactly its final size, returning the shared empty array when nothing is eligible; the pre-count is deliberately exact rather than a batch-count hint so a delete-heavy or saga-heavy batch cannot over-allocate. `ComputeElidedIndices` likewise sizes its result set from the manifest and missing-set counts, clamped at zero because the missing set arrives over the wire and is not trusted to be a subset of the manifest. (5) `ReceiverAppliedContentIndex`, maintained once per applied entry, recycles the evicted linked-list node when admitting a key into a partition already at capacity - the steady state on a warm receiver - rather than allocating a replacement and letting the evicted node become garbage; the survivor set is provably unchanged, because the recycled node is re-attached at most-recently-used exactly where a fresh one landed, and the trim pass is retained so a capacity that shrank between calls is still honoured. Measured on a new `replicationtrims` BenchmarkDotNet suite at full fidelity: the frontier encode is 46% faster and allocates 53% less, the delta decode is 34% faster at flat allocation (a probe fold is invisible to the memory diagnoser), the manifest plan is 12% faster and allocates 58% less, and the receiver index is 14% faster and allocates 53% less. (`Orleans.Lattice.Replication`)

### Fixed

- **A permanent repository-context symbol re-embed loop was traced to a WAL replay livelock in the core leaf grain; four mitigations were shipped against it and three were field-validated as ineffective.** On a saturated `repo-context-vector-membership` tree the indexer re-embedded the same ~340 symbol passages on every reconcile pass despite reporting a fully converged plan, and the memory arm's orphan sweep never ran ([#2071](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2071)). The per-pass counts were flat rather than decaying (341, 372, 340, 339), which is the signature of a stable loop rather than a converging back-fill. Four changes were shipped at the ingestor and replay-scheduling level and validated on a live deployment against a matched pre-fix baseline on the same box with the indexing cadence held constant, and **three of them did not move the symptom**. The resumable marker scan and saturation backoff ([#2072](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/2072)) banked nothing, because it resumes at *page* granularity while the stall occurs *inside* the first page, and because its backoff keys off embed batches failing while the batches in fact succeed - there were zero embedding-coverage probe failures and zero saturation triggers across the whole run. A loop detector keyed on repeated *work* rather than on failure ([#2079](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/2079)) likewise did not converge. Ordering the replay pass-1 sweep by backlog so the largest partition can drain in place ([#2091](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/2091)) was the most informative negative: in the candidate arm the affected leaf's partition held the inline-drain slot for the whole scan and its checkpoint still froze at a single offset across 37 successive activations, which is what established that the drain slot is not the binding constraint. A per-pass marker-scan counter ([#2074](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/2074)) is what made the first of those negatives legible at all, by distinguishing "resumed and still incomplete" from "finished in one call" from "never reached". Four further changes did land as genuine fixes: the gap scanner now derives has-more from the range rather than from the page fill ([#2080](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/2080)); a WAL prefix-trim probe fault is confined to its own partition instead of being read as "not trimmed" for the whole leaf ([#2082](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2082), [#2084](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/2084)); the derived indexing cadence is reported at startup with a warning when the configured combination silently disables walk pruning ([#2075](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2075), [#2092](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/2092)); and the WAL materialiser pin grain now evicts its cached bucket holder when a bucket write fails, so a stale ETag can no longer wedge a shard into an unbounded write-retry loop that Orleans' idle-based collection never clears because the loop's own traffic keeps the grain hot ([#2096](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2096), [#2097](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/2097)). The root cause is recorded as [#2098](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2098): an interrupted leaf replay banks no progress, because a partition that meets an unresolved saga prepare during replay pass 1 pins its incremental flush ceiling at `prepare - 1`, and for every partition except the last-absorbed the prepare's own terminal is deferred to pass 2 and therefore cannot resolve within that pass. `WalPartitions` defaults to 8, so this affects 7 of every 8 partitions, and it reproduces deterministically: on an identical window carrying a fully self-contained saga, a single-partition leaf converges while a two-partition leaf freezes at checkpoint 1 across 12 successive interrupted activations. **No fix is shipped for it.** The obvious repair - persisting the unresolved-prepare set alongside the checkpoint - was specified, reviewed and rejected before implementation, because the persisted checkpoint is also the WAL retention floor, so advancing it authorises WAL garbage collection to trim the very entries a resume needs and converts a slow-but-correct livelock into silent data loss; #2098 carries the two-coordinate design that resolves that interlock, the call-site map, and the open questions that must be answered before anyone implements it. Two contributing defects were separated out rather than left inside the closed investigation: a B+ leaf chain never reclaims leaves when a range shrinks, so scan cost tracks leaf count indefinitely and the affected range measured about 0.5 live rows per leaf ([#2099](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2099)); and the over-budget replay warning is throttled per (tree, leaf, partition), so its aggregate volume scales with tree size, reached 46% of all container log lines, and rolls away the evidence needed to diagnose the condition producing it ([#2100](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2100)). [#2089](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2089) and [#2078](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2078) are closed as superseded by #2098. (`Orleans.Lattice`, `Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **A work-pump coordinator started inside Orleans' reminder-service startup window no longer fails the caller's operation.** `CoordinatorGrain<TSelf>.StartCoordinatorAsync` - the shared base for the tree snapshot, resize, shard-split and reshard coordinators - registered its crash-recovery keepalive reminder with a bare `RegisterOrUpdateReminder` call. Orleans initialises its reminder service asynchronously after the silo reaches `Active`, so a coordinator that started inside that window saw the transient "Reminder Service is still initializing" fault propagate straight out and fail the operation that launched it, even though the same registration moments later would have succeeded. The registration is now wrapped in the same bounded `ReminderServiceReadiness.RetryWhileInitializingAsync` wait-out that the atomic-write saga's essential keepalive already uses, so the transient is retried across the startup window rather than surfaced; any other fault, and a transient that never clears within the retry budget, still surface with their original shape so a genuinely stuck reminder service is not mistaken for a healthy start. A `protected virtual` backoff seam lets the regression tests drive the retry budget without real delays. The affected type is `internal`, so no public surface changes. ([#2086](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2086)) (`Orleans.Lattice`)

- **A fully converged repository-context reconcile pass is now observable in the log.** The reconcile-plan line was emitted inside `if (!plan.IsNoOp || backfill.Count > 0)` and the symbol-embedding tally inside `if (symbolsEmbedded > 0)`, so a pass that legitimately measured a converged repository - zero added, zero updated, zero removed, no symbol to embed - logged nothing whatsoever, and was indistinguishable from a pass that never ran. A reader polling the log for a "0 added, 0 updated, 0 removed" convergence line was waiting for a line that by construction never appeared. The plan line is now emitted unconditionally, ahead of the apply gate that still guards the actual write (so a no-op still commits nothing), and the symbol tally now logs its zero case too. A regression test drives a converged no-op pass and asserts both lines are present while no chunk is committed. Observability only; the affected type is `internal`, so no public surface changes. ([#2088](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2088)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **The TTL-preservation integration tests no longer race a sub-second expiry against the very saga they exercise.** `MergeAsync_preserves_TTL_on_target_tree`, `Split_drain_preserves_TTL_on_target_shard` and `ResizeAsync_preserves_TTL` each wrote a key with a short (400-900 ms) TTL and then asserted the key was still live immediately after a multi-grain merge, split-drain or resize saga completed. The assertion presumed the saga finished well inside the TTL, but the TTL clock runs throughout the saga, so on a loaded runner the expiry could elapse before the post-op read and the test failed intermittently through no product fault. Each test now proves TTL preservation without a wall-clock race: liveness is asserted with a long (one-hour) TTL the saga cannot outlast, and honoured expiry is proved separately by asserting a short-TTL key reads back null only after a delay that comfortably exceeds its TTL. A new deterministic regression test delays past a short TTL *before* the resize saga runs and confirms a long-TTL sibling survives while the expired key does not, pinning the property the flaky tests meant to check. Test-only; no shipped code changes. ([#2076](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2076)) (`Orleans.Lattice`)

- **The tenant-dimension hygiene scanner no longer flags a bare identifier that merely shares a name with a metric instrument declared in an unrelated type.** `MetricEmissionScanner` gathered every instrument name across `src/` into one global set and then treated any bare `Identifier.Add(` / `Identifier.Record(` call whose identifier was in that set as a metric emission site required to carry a tenant dimension. A local variable, field or collection named the same as an instrument in some other file - a plain `Entries.Add(` list append, say - was therefore mis-detected as an untagged emission, which the gate could only be kept green against by pinning the offending name in a sentinel allow-list. Instrument discovery is now scoped per declaring type: a bare identifier counts as an emission only when an instrument of that name is declared within the same type, unioned across the partial-class files that share the `{TypeName}.{Concern}.cs` convention, so a name collision across unrelated types no longer yields a false positive. A qualified `Meter.Instrument.Add(` site is matched exactly as before. Regression tests cover a genuinely untagged bare emission (still reported), a bare name colliding with an instrument declared in another type (no longer a site), and a bare emission in a partial of the declaring type (still reported), so the narrowing cannot mask a real untagged emission. Test-only; no shipped code changes. (`Orleans.Lattice`)

- **The per-tenant WAL isolation integration fixture no longer drives an internal grain surface from the external test client.** `TenantWalPlacementClusterFixture.SystemTree` addressed `ISystemLattice` through the test cluster's external client, which is exactly the route the internal-origin assertion added to that surface refuses. The fixture's silo registers the auth add-on, so the capability-stripping filter correctly stripped the internal-origin marker from those calls and all three WAL-routing tests failed with `LatticeAuthorizationDeniedException` - a true positive against the test, not the guard. The surface is now resolved from the silo's own grain factory, which is the origin its real production caller (the registry, running in-silo) actually has, so the fixture observes the same placement machinery production does. Test-only; no shipped code changes. (`Orleans.Lattice.Tenancy`)

- **Three more CRDT records with `byte[]` payloads now compare by value.** The typed-delta `LwwRegisterDelta` (the last-writer-wins / opaque-bytes register delta) and `BoundedRegisterDelta` (the monotonic bounded-register delta, shared by the `Max` and `Min` directions), and the client-side `LatticeStagedCrdtWrite` staging token that rides a cross-tree atomic write, each carry one or more opaque `byte[]` members (`LwwRegisterDelta.Value`; `BoundedRegisterDelta.Value` and `OrderKey`; `LatticeStagedCrdtWrite.Value` and `Delta`) but relied on the compiler-generated record equality, which compares a `byte[]` with `EqualityComparer<T>.Default` (reference equality). Two structurally identical values, and in particular a delta and its post-serialization self, therefore never compared equal, so any dedup, cache lookup, or round-trip verification framed as record equality silently failed. Each now hand-writes `Equals` / `GetHashCode`, comparing the byte arrays by content, mirroring the read-surface and wire-request records hardened earlier in this series. No public surface changes: the records already expose `Equals` / `GetHashCode` / `==`. (`Orleans.Lattice`)

- **Three more gRPC wire request records with `byte[]` payloads now compare by value.** The data-plane `DataSetRequest` (the point-set request) and `CrdtWriteRequest` (the unified typed-CRDT write request), and the tree-admin `TreeAdminCreateViewRequest` (the create-materialised-view request), each carry an opaque `byte[]` member (`DataSetRequest.Value`, `CrdtWriteRequest.Element`, and `TreeAdminCreateViewRequest.Payload`) but relied on the compiler-generated record equality, which compares a `byte[]` with `EqualityComparer<T>.Default` (reference equality). Two structurally identical requests, and in particular a request and its post-serialization self, therefore never compared equal, so any dedup, cache lookup, or round-trip verification framed as record equality silently failed. Each now hand-writes `Equals` / `GetHashCode`, comparing the byte array by content, mirroring the read-surface records hardened earlier in this series - these are the write-request twins those passes did not cover. No public surface changes: the records already expose `Equals` / `GetHashCode` / `==`. (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Data.Grpc`, `Orleans.Lattice.Api.TreeAdmin.Grpc`)

- **A URL userinfo credential in a repository-context log line is now redacted even when the password carries a sub-delimiter.** The secret redactor scans a URL-like span for the userinfo/host boundary `@` so it can replace the credential with a placeholder before the text reaches a log line or a failure reason. That forward scan stopped at the first authority terminator, and its terminator set included the RFC 3986 sub-delims `,`, `;`, `)` and `'` - all of which are legal, unencoded, in a userinfo component. A remote whose userinfo carried one (a password such as `p,ss`) truncated the scan before the `@`, so no boundary was found and the whole `user:p,ss` credential was appended verbatim - the exact leak the redactor exists to prevent, and reachable through the credential-free `ScanCommit` failure path where URL redaction is the only defence. The boundary scan now stops only at a character that cannot appear unencoded in userinfo (a path, query, or fragment separator, whitespace, or a double quote), while a separate host scan still stops at a sub-delim so a following URL or a trailing token in prose is never swallowed into the redacted host. Regression tests cover a sub-delim inside the userinfo and two comma-separated URLs on one line. The affected type is `internal`, so no public surface changes. (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **Three internal grain surfaces that deliberately bypass the `ILattice` facade now assert internal origin, closing a direct-grain-call route around the access gate.** All access-gate enforcement lives on the `ILattice` facade, so the physical grains it delegates to have carried a defense-in-depth internal-origin assertion since the shard and leaf grains were hardened for [#1103](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/1103). Three surfaces reachable by the same route did not, and each was declared `internal` on the understanding that accessibility was itself the boundary. It is not: Orleans binds a grain interface by its stable `[Alias]` string rather than by CLR identity, so an external client can declare a structurally matching aliased interface and bind to the same activation. That is precisely why the capability-stripping call filter re-derives trust from the call's source at every hop instead of trusting the caller, and why the shard and leaf grains guard at all. The three gaps differ in impact, so all three are closed. (1) `ISystemLattice` is the internal read, write, delete and enumerate surface over the reserved system-tree namespace that the public facade refuses to address, which holds the auth policy tree and the replication write-ahead log, so reaching it externally was a full policy-store bypass rather than access to one tree; all six members now assert, and the two streaming members assert on the call itself rather than inside the deferred iterator body, so a caller that never enumerates is refused just the same. (2) `IReplicationApplyGrain` installs a remote mutation while preserving the authoring cluster's hybrid logical clock and origin cluster id verbatim, deliberately bypassing both the access gate and local conflict resolution so a receiver reproduces the producer's view exactly; reaching it externally let a client forge replicated writes attributed to another cluster and stamped with an attacker-chosen clock, which conflict resolution would then honour. All ten apply entry points now assert. (3) `ILatticeCrossTreeReceiverGrain.NotifyTerminalAsync` carries the commit-or-abort verdict for a whole cross-tree batch on a receiver cluster and, on the first terminal, freezes the wait set, origin cluster and operation id; it validated the shape of a terminal but authorized nothing, so an external caller could force a premature global verdict on a batch in flight and equally poison a barrier that had not started, breaking the all-or-nothing cross-tree visibility the coordinator exists to enforce. It is the receiver-side twin of the single-tree saga's `IAtomicWriteGrain.FinalizeAsync`, which was already guarded, and it is now guarded identically and ahead of the redelivery short-circuit so a settled verdict is not disclosed either. The sibling `ILatticeCrossTreeTxGrain.CommitAsync` was audited in the same sweep and left unchanged, because it already re-derives the access gate per leg against the original caller's identity before any staging. Every assertion uses the enforcement-gated overload, which resolves the `LatticeInternalOriginEnforcementMarker` sentinel from the activation's services and returns immediately when it is absent, so a cluster that has not opted into `AddLatticeAuth` pays one singleton lookup on a low-frequency coordinator path and behaves exactly as before. No public surface changes: all three interfaces are `internal`. Regression tests drive each surface by direct external grain call against a cluster with enforcement registered and assert the refusal, alongside the existing coverage proving the facade path still succeeds and that a forged internal-origin or system-origin marker is stripped from a client call. (`Orleans.Lattice`)

- **The repository-context full-walk deadline is now counted in reconcile passes, so directory-modification-time pruning actually engages on a large repository.** The previous fix widened `LATTICE_FULL_WALK_INTERVAL_SECONDS` past the widest *scheduled* reconcile spacing and expressed that as `RepoContextIndexingOptions.PruningCanEngage`. That condition is necessary but not sufficient, because the reconcile is single-flight: the real gap between two walks is the larger of the configured spacing and the previous pass's own duration. On a repository whose pass takes ten minutes against a fifteen-minute schedule the two agree, but on one whose pass takes seven minutes and is re-driven continuously they do not, and a wall-clock deadline read against that gap is already past on arrival every single time. Every pass therefore forced a full sweep and the prune snapshot was written on every run and read for nothing, exactly the inert state the previous fix set out to end - with no symptom to notice, because the index stayed correct and only the cost was wrong. Both periodic deadlines are now counted in passes rather than read from a clock: a wall-clock interval is converted once, by dividing it by the widest scheduled spacing, rounding up, and clamping to at least one pass, so the bound holds however long an individual pass takes. At the shipped defaults that is three passes to a full walk, so two reconciles in three prune, and `PruningCanEngage` is now the genuinely sufficient `PassesPerFullWalk >= 2`. Setting the interval at or below one reconcile spacing still reproduces the old "full walk every time" behaviour, but now by deliberate clamp rather than by accident. A regression test drives consecutive passes that each outlive the configured interval on a controllable clock and asserts the next one still prunes; it fails against the wall-clock deadline. The container documentation and sample, which previously told operators the invariant was about interval width, now state the pass-count rule and why pass duration is what really sets the spacing. The affected type is `internal`, so no public surface changes. ([#2048](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2048)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **Three more records with `byte[]` payloads now compare by value.** The B+ tree's `ChildDigestSnapshot` (the per-child subtree-fold contribution carried on the digest-propagation hook), the views layer's `AggregationContribution` (a single fold effect derived from one source mutation), and the repository-context portability layer's `RepoContextVectorPayload` (the optional-vector value type carried through the enumeration and snapshot surface) each hold an opaque `byte[]` payload but relied on the compiler-generated record equality, which compares a `byte[]` with `EqualityComparer<T>.Default` (reference equality). Two structurally identical values, and in particular a value and its post-serialization self, therefore never compared equal, so any dedup, cache lookup, or content comparison framed as record equality silently failed - a live hazard for `ChildDigestSnapshot`, which the `ImmutableGrainBoundaryContractTests` registry classifies as a value-equality content-digest type alongside the `LeafProjectionDigest` and `ViewDigest` siblings hardened earlier in this series, and an inconsistency for `RepoContextVectorPayload`, whose sibling `RepoContextSnapshotRecord` already compares its opaque bytes by content. Each now hand-writes `Equals` / `GetHashCode`, comparing the byte array by content. No public surface changes: all three are `readonly record struct`s or records that already expose `Equals` / `GetHashCode` / `==`, and `ChildDigestSnapshot` and `RepoContextVectorPayload` are internal. (`Orleans.Lattice`, `Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **Directory-modification-time pruning in the repository-context reconcile now actually engages.** The background reconcile keeps a per-repository snapshot of every directory's modification time so an unchanged directory can carry its known files forward without re-stating them, and a periodic full sweep catches the one change pruning cannot see: an in-place content edit, which leaves the parent directory's modification time untouched. That sweep was due every `LATTICE_FULL_WALK_INTERVAL_SECONDS`, defaulted to `300`, while two consecutive reconciles are never closer together than `LATTICE_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (`900`) plus up to `LATTICE_RECONCILE_JITTER_SECONDS` (`300`) of jitter. Every reconcile therefore arrived already past the interval and forced a full sweep, so on shipped defaults the snapshot was rebuilt on every pass and read for nothing: the optimisation was inert, and the only thing it contributed was the cost it exists to remove. There was no symptom to notice - the index stayed correct, just at full-walk cost forever. The default is now `3600`, strictly wider than the widest reconcile spacing, so roughly three reconciles in four prune. The trade is stated rather than hidden: worst-case detection latency for a pure in-place content edit moves from about 15-20 minutes to about 60-80, and an operator who wants it back can lower the interval knowing what it costs. Nothing else changes - pruning remains opt-in per request and is still applied only to the background reconcile, so an explicit `repocontext_add_repo` continues to run a full, exact walk and observe the tree immediately. The invariant is now expressed in code as `RepoContextIndexingOptions.PruningCanEngage` and pinned by a defaults test, and a behavioural regression test drives two consecutive reconciles a full reconcile-plus-jitter apart on a controllable clock and asserts the second one prunes; both fail against the previous default. The affected type is `internal`, so no public surface changes. ([#2042](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/2042)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **The repository-context workspace boundary is now enforced, not merely advertised.** `AddRepoContextTools(enableWrites: true, workspaceMode: true)` with no `workspaceRoot` - a legal call, because the parameter is optional - registered a `RepoContextWorkspaceGuard` with no roots. That guard reports itself as not enforcing and its resolver short-circuits to admit *every* path, yet workspace mode still contributed `repocontext_add_repo`, whose own published contract states that the path is "resolved against the workspace boundary; a path outside it is rejected". A caller could therefore name any directory on the host - `/`, a home directory, a secrets mount - with `respectGitignore: false` and `excludeBinary: false`, have the ingest project every file body into the content tree, and read it straight back out through `repocontext_context`, `repocontext_search` and `repocontext_outline`: an unauthenticated-at-the-tool-layer arbitrary local file read dressed as repository onboarding. The fix is fail-closed at two seams. The registration extension now computes the workspace roots *before* building the tool group and withholds `repocontext_add_repo` entirely when there are none, so advertisement matches enforcement; it deliberately does **not** substitute `repocontext_bootstrap` in its place, because bootstrap accepts an equally unbounded caller-supplied path and the fallback would reopen the same hole under a different name. `repocontext_remove_repo` and `repocontext_list_repos` are unaffected - neither takes a path. The handler then re-checks the *effective* dependency-injected guard when `repocontext_add_repo` is invoked and refuses with a clear `McpException`, which is what covers a host that registered its own non-enforcing guard despite supplying a root. Argument validation still runs first, so a malformed call reports the parameter problem rather than the boundary refusal. The single-repository `repocontext_bootstrap` surface is untouched and still accepts an arbitrary path by design: there the path is host configuration rather than caller input, and that deliberate opt-out is now pinned by test. No public surface changes. (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **A read-only MCP caller is no longer advertised the mutating repository-context tools.** `LatticeApiMcpGroupCapabilityMap.RequiredOperations` returns one coarse operation mask per facade group, and `AuthAdminMcpPermissionResolver` admits a group when *any* single `Allow` rule intersects that mask. The repository-context group's mask spans the whole data plane - `Read`, `Write`, `Delete`, `RangeRead`, `RangeDelete`, `CrdtApply`, `AtomicWrite`, `BulkLoad` - so a principal granted nothing but `Read` was admitted to the entire group, and the caller's actual operation mask was then discarded rather than consulted again. The session's advertised tool list therefore included `repocontext_remember`, `repocontext_update`, `repocontext_forget`, `repocontext_add_repo`, `repocontext_remove_repo` and `repocontext_bootstrap`, and because that same collection serves `tools/call`, they were invocable, not merely visible - a least-privilege violation that let a read grant write to and delete from the store. `LatticeApiMcpAccessSet` now also carries the union of the caller's `Allow`-granted operations, and the discovery core applies a per-tool minimum inside a group it has already admitted, via a new `ILatticeApiMcpToolGroup.RequiredOperationsFor` whose default implementation returns the group's own mask so no existing group changes behaviour. The repository-context group overrides it to demand a mutating operation for those six tools. The group masks themselves are unchanged, so the `lattice_capabilities` wire response is byte-identical and a read-only caller still legitimately sees the group for its read tools. A resolver that supplies no operation detail keeps the previous group-level-only filtering, so the refinement cannot silently narrow an existing deployment. All the affected types are `internal`; no public surface changes. (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp`, `Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **`JwtCredentialAuthenticator` no longer leaves the signature-algorithm allow-list unrestricted by default.** `JwtAuthenticatorOptions.Algorithms` is empty by default, and the authenticator pinned `TokenValidationParameters.ValidAlgorithms` only when it was non-empty. An empty `ValidAlgorithms` is treated by the token validator as "no restriction", so a deployment that configured an issuer and signing keys and never touched `Algorithms` - the shape in the package's own documentation - accepted a token whose header advertised *any* algorithm the trusted key set could satisfy, leaving the algorithm-confusion gap (CWE-347) open: a host that pinned both asymmetric and symmetric key material could be induced to verify an attacker-minted HMAC token against a symmetric key configured for an unrelated purpose. Porting the sibling `OidcCredentialAuthenticator`'s deny-all posture was not viable, because there the empty case is a discovery document that named no algorithms whereas here empty is the documented default, so deny-all would take every existing host from "authenticates" to "rejects every token" on upgrade. Instead the allow-list is now *derived* from the families of the configured signing keys: an RSA-only or EC-only key set accepts only that family's algorithms, and a symmetric-only key set accepts only the HMAC algorithms, so a deployment can never be talked into verifying against a key of the wrong family. The list is left unrestricted only where no family can be established - an empty key set, a set mixing symmetric and asymmetric material, or one containing a key type the derivation does not recognise - so a legitimately mixed host keeps working and can pin explicitly. An explicit `Algorithms` pin remains authoritative and is never widened. No public surface changes; the existing `Algorithms` and `ValidationParameters` knobs already express every override a host needs. (`Orleans.Lattice.Membership`)

- **Three read-projection records with `byte[]` payloads now compare by value.** The Explorer's `DataEntry` and `DeadLetterEntry` projections and the repository-context portability snapshot record `RepoContextSnapshotRecord` each carry an opaque `byte[]` payload - and, for the snapshot record, an optional `byte[]` vector, and for `DataEntry` a decoded member list - but relied on the compiler-generated record equality, which compares arrays and lists with `EqualityComparer<T>.Default` (reference equality). Two structurally identical records, and in particular a record and its post-serialization self, therefore never compared equal, so any dedup, cache lookup, or round-trip verification framed as record equality silently failed. Each now hand-writes `Equals` / `GetHashCode`, comparing the byte array(s) by content and the member list element by element, mirroring the state-API source records hardened earlier in this series. No public surface changes: records already expose `Equals` / `GetHashCode` / `==`, and `RepoContextSnapshotRecord` is internal. (`Orleans.Lattice.Explorer`, `Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`)

- **The platform-operator tenant-quota bar no longer rounds an exact midpoint differently from the tenant's own gauge.** `TenantQuotaRow.BarPercent` scaled the shared `Utilization` fraction to a whole-percent bar width with `Math.Round(fraction * 100d)`, which uses banker's rounding (`MidpointRounding.ToEven`), while its sibling `TenantQuotaGauge.BarPercent` in the self-service MyTenant plugin rounds the identical value with `MidpointRounding.AwayFromZero`. A tenant sitting on an exact half-percent midpoint - for example 125 of 1000, an exact 12.5% - therefore rendered a 12% bar in the operator view and a 13% bar in its own view for the same usage. The operator row now rounds away from zero to match the gauge, so both surfaces report one width for one utilization. (`Orleans.Lattice.Explorer`)

- **The Explorer's dim text colour now meets the WCAG AA contrast minimum, in both themes.** `--lx-color-text-dim` was `#5a6373` in the dark palette, which is 3.19:1 against the `#0b0e14` canvas where WCAG 2.1 AA (1.4.3) requires 4.5:1 - the relaxed 3:1 large-text allowance does not apply, because the token is spent at the body and caption steps of the type scale, all of which sit below the 18.66px / 14pt-bold threshold. It is now `#7c8797`, which clears the bar against *every* background token in the palette rather than only the canvas, the tightest pairing being 4.82:1 on `--lx-color-surface-raised`. The light palette carried a worse instance of the same defect, `#8a93a3` at 2.91:1, and is now `#656f7d` (4.79:1 on its canvas, 5.09:1 on white); the axe sweep that found the dark one could never have found it, because the sweep only ever runs the default dark theme. In both palettes the token stays strictly less emphatic than `--lx-color-text-muted`, so the de-emphasis it exists to express survives the correction. Every text/background pair in both palettes is now re-measured from the stylesheet on each build by the new `TextContrastTokenHygieneTests`, which needs no browser and therefore runs in the required `build-and-test` check rather than in the advisory browser lane; with the defect fixed and guarded, the accessibility sweep's `color-contrast` exclusion is deleted along with the allow-list mechanism that held it, so the rule now blocks unconditionally. ([#1801](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/1801)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Explorer`)

- **The Explorer now mounts correctly under a non-root base path, and its catalog renders empty, loading and error states through one shared renderer.** Sub-path hosting produced broken asset and navigation URLs because the base href was assumed to be root; the high-contrast sweep and a single catalog state renderer land with it. ([#1915](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/pull/1915)) (`Orleans.Lattice.Explorer`)

### Security

- **The three tree-admin verbs that act on a second caller-supplied identifier now authorize that identifier too, closing three paths by which a caller could reach a tree it holds no grant on.** Each of `SetTreeAliasAsync`, `SnapshotTreeAsync` and `CreateViewAsync` accepts more than one caller-named resource, composes and reserved-namespace-checks every one of them - and then authorized only the first, leaving the far side of a cross-tree operation ungoverned. That is the same shape the core already defends against at its sibling seams (`LatticeGrain.MergeAsync` authorizes its caller-supplied source through `EnforceSourceTreeReadAsync` precisely so "authorizing only the destination would let a caller holding Admin on a tree it owns siphon any other tree in the cluster", and the backup engine asserts physical-tree provenance at its own alias-swap seam), so all three are now brought into line. (1) `SetTreeAliasAsync` authorized the **logical** id but not the alias **target**. Every data-plane gate - `LatticeGrain`'s access-gate enforcement, and the request this facade's own verbs build - is evaluated against the logical id *before* the registry resolves the alias, and the physical shard and leaf grains deliberately enforce no policy of their own because they are documented as reachable only through an already-authorized logical identity; a caller holding `Admin` on a single tree it legitimately owned could therefore alias that tree onto one it held nothing on and read and rewrite the target through the ordinary `ILattice` surface, under its own tree's policy. The registry's `ThrowIfAliasEscalatesNamespace` guard is namespace-shaped (it refuses a `_lattice_` target, an ordinary-to-`sys-` crossing, and a foreign-tenant target), so two ordinary same-namespace ids passed it unconditionally - which is exactly the case now closed. (2) `SnapshotTreeAsync` authorized the **source** but not the **destination**, and a snapshot *creates* its destination in the registry (at caller-chosen structural sizing) and drains the source's whole contents into it. The core's `EnforceWholeTreeAsync(Admin)` is hard-bound to the source tree's own id and its `ThrowIfReservedSnapshotDestination` is likewise namespace-only, so an ordinary destination was ungoverned end to end: a caller could stand up a fully-populated tree at an id it had no authority over, bypassing both `CreateTreeAsync` (which authorizes the exact id being created) and `BeginBulkLoadAsync` (which authorizes the `BulkLoad` capability on it), and pre-plant forged content at an id a victim's policy grants them but which they have not created yet. (3) `CreateViewAsync` authorized the caller's chosen **source** but never the **incumbent** registration behind the caller-supplied view name, and view registration is last-write-wins at both layers (`ViewRegistryGrain.RegisterAsync` overwrites the durable record, `ViewCatalog.Register` the entry). A caller holding `Admin` on any tree it owned could therefore re-point an established view at its own source: the maintainer then projected attacker-controlled data into the existing `view-` tree, which the caller could not write to directly because the maintainer runs under a system-origin bypass, and the rightful owner was locked out of `RebuildViewAsync` / `ReconcileViewAsync` / `DropViewAsync`, all of which authorize against the *resolved* source. A first-time create and an idempotent re-create over the same source are unaffected; only a genuine rebind now requires `Admin` on the source the view currently tails as well as on the one it is moving to. All three fixes are purely additive authorization calls at the facade seam - no public API signature changed, and no previously-authorized call is now refused. (`Orleans.Lattice.Api.TreeAdmin`)

## Released

Published releases, newest first. Each section is keyed by its publish date; within a date, packages advance on their own patch digits per [`docs/RELEASING.md`](docs/RELEASING.md).

## [2026-09-05]

Coordinated family minor release - every published package outside the Explorer sub-family advances in lockstep to `9.6.0`. The headline is the shard-root contention series, now bounded end to end: every leaf-chain walk that could hold a deliberately non-reentrant shard root for the length of a chain is now bounded by the work it does rather than by the output it collects, resumes by key rather than by leaf grain id so a leaf split or reclaimed between batches cannot silently truncate a sweep, and sits under a hard end-to-end stall ceiling for the prologue and descent phases a cooperative budget structurally cannot reach - with the handful of walks that must stay atomic now recording why at each site and warning when they hold a shard past a threshold. Alongside it: a broad hot-path allocation and hash-probe trimming pass across the view-maintainer drain, slot routing, reshard slot accounting, the tenancy merge and the CRDT delta combine; a set of delimiter-aliasing fixes that make the caller-identity, projection-version and secret-set signatures injective; a WAL materialiser pin store that no longer rewrites every co-routed leaf on each flush, now bucketed and observable in the saturation signal; and the documentation corpus is now published as a browsable website.

One package debuts in this line, entering at the family version `9.6.0`: `Orleans.Lattice.Storage.File`, a durable local-disk write-ahead-log backend - an append-and-fsync log per shard with crash-safe reconciliation and background compaction that rewrites the log to reclaim trimmed space - intended for single-node and containerized deployments.

**The Explorer sub-family remains deliberately held back at `9.4.0` / `9.4.1` and does not participate in this lockstep**, for the same reason it was held back at `9.5.0`: the console rewrite ([#1790](https://github.com/NSTA1/Orleans.Lattice/issues/1790)) removes public types from the published `Orleans.Lattice.Explorer.Core` and would require minting thirteen new plugin package ids, so the whole sub-family ships together in the next major instead. Its entries remain under `## Unreleased`. Because no shipping package references an Explorer project, holding it back leaves no unresolvable dependency.

Three further companion packages remain **not yet published to NuGet** (build from source today): `Orleans.Lattice.Vector`, `Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext`, and `Orleans.Lattice.Api.Mcp.RepoContext.Replication`.

### Added

- **The documentation is now a browsable website, published to GitHub Pages.** The whole documentation corpus, the runnable samples and their source, and the root README, feature catalogue and package inventory are rendered as one searchable site: full-text search, navigation grouped exactly as `PACKAGES.md` groups packages by seam and `FEATURES.md` groups capabilities by concern, syntax-highlighted snippets, and rendered Mermaid diagrams. Every sample gains a generated Source page, so a sample's `Program.cs` is one click from its README exactly as it is when browsing the repository, and the two largest samples list their remaining files rather than inlining a page nobody can read. Nothing about the in-repo Markdown changes: the site is generated from it, so the repository stays the source of truth and the docs remain readable on github.com.
